H.3 Visualization of failure-guided retrieval . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . 42 42 45 46 46 47 48 49 50 50Execution during deployment generates experience that robots can use to improve policy reliability. Robot foundation models provide initial capabilities by translating visual and language understanding into physical action [3, 4, 11, 20, 44], but these initial capabilities do not automatically yield reliable execution [5, 19, 31]. Variations in object properties, contact conditions and individual robots expose gaps in these capabilities, particularly when errors accumulate over multi-stage tasks [21, 43]. At the same time, robots operating in the real world generate experience that can support further policy improvement [1, 6]. As deployment expands to larger fleets, parallel execution provides growing source of physical experience [16, 17, 22, 32, 36]. The question is how to turn this growing experience into more reliable task execution during deployment. central constraint is the human effort required to supply corrective actions during deployment. Expert demonstrations and corrective interventions have proved useful for improving policies during real-world execution [1, 13, 18, 24, 25, 34]. Recent fleet-learning systems combine parallel collection with human supervision to connect robot execution and policy updates [14, 32, 36]. When learning depends on new expert actions, teleoperators must remain available to monitor execution, recognize failures and take control to provide appropriate corrections. As the deployed fleet grows, the cap"

Text:"MemT-1.0: Towards Memory-Native EvolutionMemory-native evolution requires making memory an intrinsic capability of foundation models, enabling them to continuously accumulate and utilize information. To this end, we introduce MemT-1.0, new architecture of memory foundation models that internalize memory as part of model computation. Building on Metis, MemT-1.0 employs Gated DeltaNet-2 with stable token-level memory update mechanism within each turn. We further introduce Mixture-of-Storage to scale native memory capacity. Following deliberate backbone preparation, we develop two-stage training pipeline consisting of continued pre-training and post-training, supported by 34B tokens of training data and efficient training and inference infrastructure. We release model family comprising MemT-1.0-Nano and MemT-1.0-Flash, and conduct comprehensive evaluation of memory and general capabilities. The results show that they outperform the parametric memory baselines on average and remain competitive with retrieval-augmented methods, while largely preserving general capabilities. At history length of 128K tokens, MemT-1.0-Nano achieves approximately 1.86 end-to-end inference speedup and 66 reduction in persistent storage relative to full context KV cache. By internalizing retrieved procedural experience, MemT-1.0 improves task success on agentic tasks, providing practical step toward memory-native evolution. Code: https://github.com/MemTensor/MemT-1.0 Model: https://huggingface.co/collections/MemTensor/memt-1 Figure 1 Memory performance of MemT-1.0 on representative benchmarks. 1 Figure 2 Agentic performance of MemT-1.0-Flash after backbone preparation without native memory. However, most existing agent memory systems construct memory in query-agnostic manner, which can incur unnecessary preprocessing cost and discard details that later prove essential. Recent studies have begun shifting memory processing toward runtime adaptation, but typically specialize in particular operations or fixed processing schemes, leaving flexible control over performance, cost, and latency largely underexplored. To address this challenge, we present MemPilot, flexible framework that orchestrates on-demand memory curation under different performancecostlatency preferences. Specifically, we optimize multi-step LLM policy via reinforcement learning to iteratively choose between retrieving from query-agnostic memory and delegating query-specific curation of raw multimodal history to heterogeneous LLMs and VLMs. The policy jointly controls evidence amount, curation instructions, model selection, and visual access, enabling fine-grained allocation of runtime computation. To optimize this policy under competing objectives, we adapt objective-wise advantage decoupling by separately estimating each objectives advantage before aggregation. Moreover, we introduce prefix-based marginal utility estimation for fine-grained credit assignment across multi-step rollouts. Experiments on five multimodal agent-memory benchmarks demonstrate favorable performancecostlatency trade-offs across optimization preferences, with preference sweeps yielding broader frontiers than existing trade-off-aware baseline"

Text:"Fr echet Forcing: Training Long-Horizon Streaming World Models from Short Clips Yian Zhao, Yu Yan, Hongcan Guo, Ruochong Zheng, Jian Zhang, Jie Chen Abstract Streaming video world models generate the future chunk by chunk, conditioned on their own past and stream of actions. When trained on short clips, they drift once rollout exceeds the training horizon, as prediction errors re-enter the context and compound. common remedy is to train the model on its own rollouts via distribution matching distillation (DMD). For action-conditioned world models, however, the teacher becomes bottleneck: an external video model is agnostic to the actions, whereas the models own pretrained network drifts in the same manner as the student. We observe that long rollout need not match teacher, provided it remains realistic continuation of its own scene that follows the commanded actions. Realism and scene fidelity can be measured directly against real-data statistics, which remain valid at any horizon, while action following is preserved by the models conditional generation loss. Building on this insight, we propose Fréchet Forcing, which fine-tunes world model together with its deployed multi-step sampler under three complementary constraints. Generative anchoring applies the flow-matching loss after self-generated history ending on real data, preserving action-conditioned generation. Context fidelity, Fréchet distance (FD) on latent statistics of the free rollout centred on each clips own ground truth, keeps every rollout faithful to its scene. Reality matching, an FD to real frames in frozen perceptual space, keeps the generated frames realistic. Because all three targets are derived from short real clips without comparing rollout against long recorded future, training requires no teacher, no online critic and no long videos. Fine-tuning MiniWorld-1B, pretrained on 253-frame clips, for at most 36 GPU-hours on 381-frame clips yields rollouts that remain stable beyond 4,000 frames and "

Text:"Fr echet Forcing: Training Long-Horizon Streaming World Models from Short Clips Yian Zhao, Yu Yan, Hongcan Guo, Ruochong Zheng, Jian Zhang, Jie Chen Abstract Streaming video world models generate the future chunk by chunk, conditioned on their own past and stream of actions. When trained on short clips, they drift once rollout exceeds the training horizon, as prediction errors re-enter the context and compound. common remedy is to train the model on its own rollouts via distribution matching distillation (DMD). For action-conditioned world models, however, the teacher becomes bottleneck: an external video model is agnostic to the actions, whereas the models own pretrained network drifts in the same manner as the student. We observe that long rollout need not match teacher, provided it remains realistic continuation of its own scene that follows the commanded actions. Realism and scene fidelity can be measured directly against real-data statistics, which remain valid at any horizon, while action following is preserved by the models conditional generation loss. Building on this insight, we propose Fréchet Forcing, which fine-tunes world model together with its deployed multi-step sampler under three complementary constraints. Generative anchoring applies the flow-matching loss after self-generated history ending on real data, preserving action-conditioned generation. Context fidelity, Fréchet distance (FD) on latent statistics of the free rollout centred on each clips own ground truth, keeps every rollout faithful to its scene. Reality matching, an FD to real frames in frozen perceptual space, keeps the generated frames realistic. Because all three targets are derived from short real clips without comparing rollout against long recorded future, training requires no teacher, no online critic and no long videos. Fine-tuning MiniWorld-1B, pretrained on 253-frame clips, for at most 36 GPU-hours on 381-frame clips yields rollouts that remain stable beyond 4,000 frames and continue to follow their actions in both robot manipulation (DROID) and camera-controlled exploration (Sekai). On DROID, PSNR against forward-backward episode replay stays above 21 dB through frame 4,000, compared with 8.9 dB for the base model. An analysis of the training dynamics further reveals how distribution matching on free rollouts fails and how each design choice prevents these failure modes. Code: https://github.com/zhao-yian/Frechet_Forcing Models: https://huggingface.co/zhaoyian01/Frechet_Forcing Page & Demo: https://zhao-yian.github.io/Frechet_Forcing Figure 1 4,000-frame chunk-by-chunk rollouts from single real frame on two DROID episodes (top four rows) and one Sekai scene (bottom two rows), following the commanded robot or camera trajectory. MiniWorld-1B, pretrained on 253-frame clips, cannot sustain longer rollouts: on DROID, saturated lattice texture spreads from the moving arm within 1,000 frames, and on Sekai the rollout darkens within the first 250 frames and then fades to black. MiniWorld-1B-FF, fine-tuned with Fréchet Forcing on 381-frame clips, stays coherent to frame 4,000, over 10 the fine-tuning horizon. 1 Introduction World models that generate video in streaming fashion, with each chunk conditioned on previously generated chunks and on an action, provide natural substrate for interactive simulation, game engines and embodied agents [1, 5, 6, 10, 15, 34, 45]. However, because they are trained on ground-truth history and deployed autoregressively on their own predictions [4], small errors re-enter the context and compound over time. Rollouts therefore remain coherent for roughly the length of the training clips before drifting, fading to black or dissolving into texture  (Fig. 1)  . This long-horizon drift remains the central obstacle to sustained streaming generation [7, 15, 40]. 2 The prevailing strategy to close this train-test gap is to train the model on its own rollouts. Because self-generated rollout has no paired ground truth beyond the first few seconds, supervision must be distributional, and recent work has largely converged on distribution matching distillation (DMD) [15, 4345], where the difference between frozen teachers score and an online fake score of the student on noised student samples provides the training gradient. State-of-the-art systems reach minute-scale video by extending DMD to longer rollouts, rolling windows and chunk-wise scoring [7, 8, 24, 40]. Teacher-free alternatives instead retain per-sample regression target by conditioning the flow-matching or diffusion loss on history corrupted by the models own errors [9, 20, 41], or by reversing rollouts so that they end on or recover the ground-truth start [14, 29]. For action-conditioned world models, however, the teacher poses fundamental dilemma. An effective teacher must simultaneously model the students domain and action space while remaining more accurate than the student on the states the student visits. large external video model violates the first requirement, as it is agnostic to the action signal and pulls rollouts towards mismatched distribution. Conversely, the world models own pretrained network satisfies the first requirement but violates the second: it is the very model whose rollouts drift, so on long-horizon states its score agrees with the student and provides no corrective signal. In our experiments, neither choice improved over the base model across broad range of configurations. Per-sample teacher-free objectives avoid the teacher altogether, yet they supervise only states that can be paired with ground truth, where degraded history is followed by its recorded continuation or reversed rollout recovers its recorded start. Because the regression target always lies within recorded clip, the states rollout visits thousands of frames later are never supervised directly. We take different view of the supervision required for long rollouts. Rather than matching teacher or paired recording, rollout must remain realistic continuation of its own scene while following the commanded actions. These properties call for complementary training signals. Visual realism and scene fidelity are distributional properties of the rollout that can be evaluated against real data at any horizon, because the distribution of real frames is fixed, compact to summarise and, unlike learned score, equally valid one second or one minute into rollout. Action following, by contrast, is property of conditional generation that the "

Text:"Moreno DIncà Nicu Sebe Massimiliano ManciniSafety in Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) depends on unsafe multimodal data for post-training alignment or activation steering. However, such data is hard to collect, must be gathered anew for each modality, and its collection and distribution raise ethical and legal concerns. In this work, we relax this constraint and investigate whether textual refusal directions, extracted directly from the LLM backbone, generalize across modalities (i.e., image, video). Preliminary findings confirm this ability, though effectiveness is conditioned by layer selection, steering strength, and cross-modal alignment, with the latter causing safe multimodal inputs to be spuriously steered toward refusal. Building on this, we introduce Modality-Agnostic Refusal Steering (MARS), lightweight training-free approach that injects multimodal safety without the need for multimodal safety data. MARS corrects modality misalignment via activation centering, adaptively scales steering strength within geometrically defined trust region, and selects the optimal intervention layer, operating at the first generated token. Evaluated on five SOTA MLLMs across safety, utility, and video jailbreak, MARS achieves consistent safety gains while preserving utility up to over-refusal. MARS reveals that safety-relevant structure is shared across modalities and that textual refusal directions are powerful and underexplored foundation for multimodal alignment. Warning: This paper contains examples of unsafe and potentially disturbing content.The capability of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) to refuse unsafe queries (denoted as safety [1, 46, 30]) is not innate but requires post-training alignment of the LLM backbone on unsafe inputs [25, 35, 36]. However, while safety alignment is standard practice in the language domain, unsafe multimodal training data is substantially harder to collect: it must capture meaningful cross-modal interactions [39, 16, 49], and it must be collected anew for every new modality (e.g., videos [24]). Therefore, MLLMs inherit safety from their LLM backbone with little dedicated multimodal safety training. As consequence, the vulnerabilities of post-training strategies, e.g., to adversarial prompting [30, 42], narrow fine-tuning [3], and circumvention strategies [1, 28, 17], are amplified in multimodal settings, where visual complexity further weakens safety guarantees [23, 24, 47, 20, 10]. At the same time, the community increasingly urges against collecting and distributing unsafe multimodal data due to ethical and legal concerns (e.g., nonconsensual or child-abuse imagery) [8, 14], and calls for safety methods that do not require unsafe multimodal data [8, 14]. In parallel, recent studies reveal that LLM safety is governed by structured signals in the activation space: refusal direction drives rejection of harmful requests [1, 46], something that can be exploited to jailbreak the model. While these findings suggest that models already contain latent safety-relevant structure, extracting them still relies on the presence of unsafe data, harder to collect for MLLMs. Correspondence to: moreno.dinca@unitn.it. Code at https://github.com/Moreno98/MARS. 40th Conference on Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS 2026). In this paper, we take different perspective, exploring whether latent (textual) safety representations transfer across modalities. If so, we can harness them to improve safety in MLLMs without relying on multimodal safety data or additional training. To this end, we revisit refusal directions as safety mechanism: given an input, we project internal activations onto the refusal direction to estimate the models implicit safety belief. We then steer the activations toward this belief, reinforcing refusal. preliminary study across multiple MLLMs shows that refusal directions extracted from text-only data generalize to multimodal inputs, suggesting the existence of shared safety-relevant structure. While encouraging, the efficacy of the approach is not universal, and it is conditioned on the steering layer and strength. Moreover, we uncover deeper cause of failure: modality misalignment. In fact, modality-specific components may be systematically aligned with the refusal direction regardless of their safety, causing safe multimodal inputs to be spuriously steered toward refusal. We address these challenges in three steps. First, we overcome the systematic modality misalignment by re-centering activations via randomly colored images, which suffice in extracting modality-relevant features without injecting priors or the need to collect data. Second, we exploit the geometry of this re-centered space to dynamically adjust the steering strength, confining intervention to the region spanned by the textual safe and unsafe centroids. Third, we select the steering layer by combining priors on the separability of unsafe representations and generalization to unseen text data. We name our lightweight, training-free approach Modality-Agnostic Refusal Steering (MARS). Experimental results on five models and seven benchmarks, spanning multiple modalities (i.e., images, videos, and text) show that MARS leads to significant safety improvements (e.g., +59.4% refusal on video jailbreaking on QWEN3-VL) without additional multimodal supervision or retraining. This suggests that current models already possess substantial, yet underutilized, safety capabilities, opening the door to lightweight and scalable alternatives to traditional alignment pipelines. The core contributions of this work are: We revisit latent refusal directions as means to increase model safety, amplifying refusal based on internal activations, enhancing safety without multimodal safety data or retraining. We provide the first analysis of textual refusal directions in multimodal models, showing that textual safety representations generalize across modalities but are conditioned on three fundamental factors: layer selection, intervention strength, and multimodal misalignment. We propose MARS to address these challenges via activation re-centering, dynamic steering, and layers importance estimation. The results uncover MARS as strong, implicit safety baseline within MLLMs that, without multimodal safety data, achieves substantial gains even in scenarios where training-based approaches may fail, such as videos.In this section, we first specify the problem of safety on Multimodal Larg"

Text:"Learning to Discriminate Scene Structures Makes Self-Supervised Depth Learning ScalableZizhan Guo Wei Ye Rui Fan(cid:66)Figure 1: Beyond (a) achieving SoTA performance across diverse datasets with unified model, MaSS is the first SSMDE method to (b) effectively benefit from increased data diversity and (c) exhibit remarkable generalizability to entirely out-of-distribution scenes.Self-supervised monocular depth estimation is appealing for its potential to scale with unlabeled data, yet existing methods often degrade when trained on diverse scenes. This study finds that, under self-supervision, models tend to encode datasetspecific structural biases rather than transferable scene geometry, leading to crossdomain optimization conflicts. To address this issue, this study proposes the MaSS framework, aiming to make self-supervised depth learning scalable to diverse data. MaSS introduces dedicated structural representation pathway explicitly decoupled from contextual features, which is guided by novel scene-structure discrimination objective to learn generalized, layout-aware structural representations for depth decoding. Trained and evaluated on four diverse datasets, both individually and jointly, MaSS substantially outperforms prior self-supervised methods and is the first to consistently benefit from mixed-domain training. Zero-shot evaluation on eight additional datasets further demonstrates its remarkable generalizability, particularly on those entirely out-of-distribution scenes. The source code is publicly available at https://mias.group/MaSS. (cid:66) Corresponding author. 40th Conference on Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS 2026).The fundamental advantage of self-supervised learning lies in its potential to leverage ubiquitous unlabeled data for scalable model training. However, most existing self-supervised monocular depth estimation (SSMDE) research [1, 2, 3, 4, 5] is predominantly confined to training models individually on single datasets or scenarios, leaving much of this potential unexplored. Recent efforts have attempted to directly train models with existing SSMDE methods on large-scale video sequences curated from online platforms [6, 7], as well as to incorporate vision foundation models (VFMs) as backbones [8, 7]. Despite these advances, overall performance improvements remain limited. As demonstrated by our preliminary experiments (see Fig. 1(b)), when existing methods are trained simultaneously on diverse monocular video data (e.g., indoor, outdoor, dynamic, and adverse weather scenarios), they fail to benefit mutually from the increased diversity and do not exhibit improved generalization to out-of-distribution scenes. Instead, we observe performance decline compared to models trained individually on single dataset. This observation demonstrates that existing SSMDE methods have not yet achieved effective scalability to diverse data. This study begins by investigating the challenges that arise when SSMDE is exposed to diverse data distributions during training. It is worth noting that the novel-view synthesis-based supervision inevitably introduces noisy and artifact-laden supervisory signals in scenarios involving occlusions, textureless regions, and illumination changes [9, 10]. Even setting aside such noise, the gradients induced by self-supervised losses often fail to reflect depth errors faithfully [4]. As result, the optimization process is inherently prone to local minima, leading to poor convergence stability [4]. Although these challenges frequently occur during self-supervised training, existing methods can still achieve reasonable accuracy on single, homogeneous dataset [3]. This is largely because the network can exploit the structural bias inherent to that dataset, thereby compensating for weak supervision. The key evidence we find is that under the existing SSMDE framework, the coarsest, high-level feature of the depth encoder does not capture depth-related cues, such as the foregroundbackground relation and perspective geometry. Instead, it primarily reflects the dataset-specific structural biases. When training on diverse datasets, different data domains often require the model to learn inconsistent structural biases for their respective convergence, leading to the observed performance drop. These findings motivate us to explicitly guide the network to learn robust, generalized scene-structure representation for depth decoding. Based on this core idea, we develop the MaSS framework that Make Self-supervised depth learning Scalable to diverse data. MaSS bridges conventional depth encoder with frozen pretrained VFM through bidirectional interaction architecture, drawing inspiration from [11]. In this design, the depth encoder focuses on extracting geometric cues that are difficult to recover from VFM features [8]. The interacted VFM features are then fed into two feature projectors to reconstruct complementary, collapse-free representations under explicit constraints and learning objectives. Specifically, one projector is adapted to learn contextual representation informative for depth estimation, such as object-level spatial relationships, while retaining generalized semantics with the original VFM features as anchors. The other projector is then constrained to be decoupled from the contextual one, thereby reserving its representational capacity for scene structure. To explicitly define this representation, we introduce scene-structure discrimination objective via contrastive learning. This objective organizes the feature space based on scene layout similarity, which is measured by the histogram distribution of predicted depth. Driven by this objective, the second projector learns transferable structural representations that capture global scene layout and perspective geometry, rather than dataset-specific biases. Together, these two complementary representations enable more reliable depth predictions while reducing their reliance on dataset-specific biases. Extensive experiments demonstrate that MaSS outperforms previous state-of-the-art (SoTA) selfsupervised methods by large margin (as presented in Fig. 1(a)) and achieves accuracy comparable to certain supervised depth foundation models. Moreover, MaSS represents the first self-supervised approach to achieve clear performance gains from increasing the diversity of training data (Fig. 1(b)), resulting in notable generalizability improvements particularly on out-of-distribution scen"

Text:"ECHO: Efficient Causal Self-Recursion Beyond Auxiliary Multi-Token Prediction Yuxuan Zhou1, Junyuan Shang1, Tianyu Wang2, Chunrui Zheng1, Zichang Liu1, Wenbin Chang1, Shuohuan Wang1, Dianhai Yu1 2Independent Researcher 1ERNIE Team, Baidu https://github.com/ernie-research/ECHO Abstract Existing Multi-token prediction (MTP) methods are auxiliary by design: the multihorizon loss mostly trains modules attached to the backbone, and each added horizon costs another module and another pass. We propose Efficient Causal Self-Recursion (ECHO), which removes both costs. The backbones own final layer predicts every horizon, so the supervision lands on the parameters that generate the next token, and Dynamic Depth Sampling (DDS) keeps deeper horizons affordable by drawing the training depth with probabilities that decay along depth. Future prediction thus becomes part of the autoregressive objective rather than task attached to it, and the gains follow on both sides of it. In pretraining, added depth converts into downstream accuracy where auxiliary modules saturate, concentrating on reasoning and code at half the MTP training overhead of training every depth. At inference, the pretrained model is its own drafter: it drafts beyond the depth it was trained for, and keeps its draft-target alignment on the open-ended, high-entropy generation where post-hoc drafters lose theirs. The advantage survives doubled token budget, repeated pretraining run, and supervised finetuning. Oct 17, 2026 Project lead. 1 ECHO: Efficient Causal Self-Recursion Oct 17, Large language models (LLMs) (Achiam et al., 2023; Touvron et al., 2023; Bai et al., 2023; Liu et al., 2024a) are predominantly trained with the Next-Token Prediction (NTP) objective. While NTP has demonstrated remarkable scalability and effectiveness, it provides supervision only at the immediate next-token horizon, leaving potentially useful future information unexploited. Moreover, autoregressive decoding remains inherently sequential,"

Text:"A Conditional No-Hair Theorem for Transformers: How Language Models Forget Their Prompt October 2026 Abstract black hole retains only few conserved charges of the matter it formed from. We ask how much an autoregressive transformer retains of its prompt after generating tokens. Consider two different prompts followed by the same continuation text, so that any difference in their hidden states comes from the prompt alone, and we find that the distance between their hidden states decays approximately as power law tα, where α = 1 is the rate at which fixed prompts share of uniform attention shrinks and smaller values mean longer memory. Beyond the first layers, trained models forget fast (α 0.550.7), while untrained models with the same architecture forget slowly (α 0.20.5, decreasing with depth). We prove that this offset is O(cid:0)P (log t)L1/t(cid:1) for prompt of length and depth L. The bound holds for finite-depth pre-LN softmax transformers with full causal attention and bounded attention logits (e.g. rotary position encodings), under one additional condition: the pre-norm activations stay away from zero. The mechanism is geometric: the prompts direct influence is diluted as the context grows, and relay continuation tokens passing on what earlier tokens read from the prompt can only add logarithmic factors. This is what we call the conditional no-hair theorem: given the text generated so far, the prompts influence on the next token vanishes as grows, so any property of the prompt that survives, such as its language, must be carried by the text itself. An exactly solvable linear relay model shows that the bound cannot be improved and reproduces the rise of the local exponent towards 1 seen in untrained networks. Cancelling the dilution with logit bias on the prompt stops the decay early in training and makes the offset grow after training, so dilution is necessary for the forgetting we measure; untrained networks and the solvable model show that it is also suffic"

Text:"A Conditional No-Hair Theorem for Transformers: How Language Models Forget Their PromptOctober 2026 Abstract black hole retains only few conserved charges of the matter it formed from. We ask how much an autoregressive transformer retains of its prompt after generating tokens. Consider two different prompts followed by the same continuation text, so that any difference in their hidden states comes from the prompt alone, and we find that the distance between their hidden states decays approximately as power law tα, where α = 1 is the rate at which fixed prompts share of uniform attention shrinks and smaller values mean longer memory. Beyond the first layers, trained models forget fast (α 0.550.7), while untrained models with the same architecture forget slowly (α 0.20.5, decreasing with depth). We prove that this offset is O(cid:0)P (log t)L1/t(cid:1) for prompt of length and depth L. The bound holds for finite-depth pre-LN softmax transformers with full causal attention and bounded attention logits (e.g. rotary position encodings), under one additional condition: the pre-norm activations stay away from zero. The mechanism is geometric: the prompts direct influence is diluted as the context grows, and relay continuation tokens passing on what earlier tokens read from the prompt can only add logarithmic factors. This is what we call the conditional no-hair theorem: given the text generated so far, the prompts influence on the next token vanishes as grows, so any property of the prompt that survives, such as its language, must be carried by the text itself. An exactly solvable linear relay model shows that the bound cannot be improved and reproduces the rise of the local exponent towards 1 seen in untrained networks. Cancelling the dilution with logit bias on the prompt stops the decay early in training and makes the offset grow after training, so dilution is necessary for the forgetting we measure; untrained networks and the solvable model show that it is also sufficient. An attention path decomposition shows that training suppresses relay within the first billion tokens, before attention sinks form. mild version of this compensation tends to raise adherence to system instructions at small perplexity cost, though no more than constant boost of equal mean strength. We will release the toolkit used in this paper, which measures α for any Hugging Face causal language model.Think of conversation that runs long with your friends. You open with remark about the weather Wednesday looks nice, say, or Thursday looks nice and by the time it has moved through work, film, friends illness, nobody could tell from the last ten minutes which of the two you said. The opening line is still in the room, in the sense that nothing was deleted. It has simply stopped mattering. Large language models behave the same way. system prompt sits at the start of the context, all of its tokens visible to every later query, and yet over few dialogue turns the persona it sets drifts measurably [Li et al., 2024]. An agent whose constraints are given once, at the start of long trajectory, undergoes the same erosion. Nothing is truncated, 1 and still the model stops listening. We ask how fast this erosion occurs, and what drives it. We find that the decay is close to power law, and that counterintuitively the training process itself accelerates this amnesia, causing trained network to forget earlier constraints faster than random-weight baseline. Whatever information does persist, such as the language of the prompt, survives not because the network retains memory of the past, but because that past has already been inscribed into the local geometry of the generated text. Why should an opening remark fade at all, when it never leaves the context? Physics offers template. black hole forgets almost everything about the matter that formed it: an exterior observer can only measure few charges, and these persist because the exterior field itself can carry them. The corresponding questions for transformer are then clear. The core inquiry of this paper is simple to state. Give language model prompt, let it write tokens, and compare two prompts that differ in single word, in phrasing, in language, or in genre. Long after the prompt, can anything inside the model still tell them apart? And if not, why not? We call the residual amount of the prompt in the hidden state its imprint, and ask three questions about it: how large is the imprint after generated tokens, how fast does it decay, and what properties of the prompt can still be read off from it. Whether an imprint of given size is readable depends on how it compares with the variation that the continuation itself causes, so we also track decodability ratio: the imprint measured against the spread of hidden states across different continuations. The two quantities are not the same, but they are linked decoder can read the prompt off the state only while the imprint stands out against the continuation and we show in what follows that in practice they decay at the same rate, which lets single exponent summarise how quickly prompt is forgotten. This paper answers these questions in three steps. We first define quantity that can be measured: the prompt offset, the difference between the hidden states of two runs that share continuation but differ in their prompt. We then prove that, under mild conditions on the architecture, this offset decays at least as fast as t1 up to logarithmic factors, and show that the bound is attained by model that can be solved exactly. Finally, we trace where the prompts influence goes as it decays: through the attention path we call relay, which the training process suppresses within the first billion tokens. Contributions. The imprint is measured as prompt offset, and five results characterise how it decays. 1. The measured decay. We define the teacher-forced prompt offset and show that it decays approximately as tα, with logarithmic corrections in untrained networks and close to pure power law in trained Pythia on coherent text. The spread across continuations stays essentially constant (β 0 except in untrained Qwen), so the rate at which the prompt is forgotten is governed by single exponent (Section 3). 2. theoretical bound. For finite-depth pre-LN transformers with non-degenerate pre-norm activations, the offset is O(cid:0)P (log t)L1/t(cid:1); conditional on the generated text, the prompts influence on the next token vanishes at the same rate."

Text:"Sparse Feature Policy Unlearning Mitigates State Hallucination in Vision-Language-Action Models Jiho Lee1, Jeongeun Park2, Heayoun Choi1, Taekyung Kim3,, and Eunwoo Kim1, Abstract Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models have shown strong generalization in robotic manipulation by leveraging rich representations from pretrained vision-language models. However, their deployment in real-world environments remains limited by recurring unreliable behaviors. In this work, we study state hallucination, recurring failure pattern in which VLA continues acting as if an unrealized robot-object state had been achieved. Our analyses find that state hallucination coincides with weakened attention to task-relevant visual regions, and mechanistic interpretation via sparse autoencoders reveals that hallucination-associated sparse features are activated when these failures occur. Based on this analysis, we propose SOUL (Sparse feature pOlicy UnLearning), which selectively unlearns policy knowledge associated with state hallucination behaviors, where sparse features identified from hallucination failures and successful behaviors serve as explicit forgetting and retention targets, respectively. Experiments across VLA architectures in simulated and real-world environments show that our method substantially reduces hallucinated failures and improves task success without substantially compromising the existing manipulation capabilities. These results suggest that interpretable feature analysis provides practical basis for selectively modifying undesirable knowledge in robot policies. I. INTRODUCTION Vision-language-action models (VLAs) have emerged as promising approach for bringing foundation models into robot manipulation [1], [2], [3]. Abstract MemT-1.0 introduces a memory-native architecture that embeds continuous memory into foundation model computation, improving long-context efficiency and agentic task performance.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Memory-native evolution requires making memory an intrinsic capability of foundation models, enabling them to continuously accumulate and utilize information. To this end, we introduce MemT-1.0, a new architecture of memory foundation models that internalize memory as part of model computation. Building on Metis, MemT-1.0 employs Gated DeltaNet-2 with a stable token-level memory update mechanism within each turn. We further introduce Mixture-of-Storage to scale native memory capacity. Following a deliberate backbone preparation, we develop a two-stage training pipeline consisting of continued pre-training and post-training, supported by 34B tokens of training data and efficient training and inference infrastructure. We release a model family comprising MemT-1.0-Nano and MemT-1.0-Flash, and conduct a comprehensive evaluation of memory and general capabilities. The results show that they outperform the parametric memory baselines on average and remain competitive with retrieval-augmented methods, while largely preserving general capabilities. At a history length of 128K tokens, MemT-1.0-Nano achieves approximately 1.86times end-to-end inference speedup and a 66times reduction in persistent storage relative to full context KV cache. By internalizing retrieved procedural experience, MemT-1.0 improves task success on agentic tasks, providing a practical step toward memory-native evolution.

Модель использует Gated DeltaNet-2 с устойчивым механизмом обновления памяти на уровне токенов и Mixture-of-Storage для масштабирования ёмкости памяти. Обучение проходит в два этапа: continued pre-training и post-training на 34B токенах. Building on Metis, MemT-1.0 employs Gated DeltaNet-2 with a stable token-level memory update mechanism within each turn. We further introduce Mixture-of-Storage to scale native memory capacity. Abstract SpecMRL improves compressed embeddings by reorganizing features via a learned spectral basis that prioritizes informative directions, outperforming standard MRL at reduced dimensions.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Matryoshka Representation Learning (MRL) is designed to support flexible representation sizes by jointly optimizing nested prefixes. While effective, this design lacks an explicit constraint to ensure that the leading portion provides a suitable compact encoding. To address this, we propose SpecMRL, which reorganizes the feature space to enable more effective prefix readout. Specifically, we extend MRL with a parameter-derived spectral basis organized with feature importance. SpecMRL otherwise follows standard MRL, with prefix readout performed in the learned spectral space. Experiments on MTEB show that SpecMRL outperforms MRL at all evaluated reduced dimensions, with larger gains under more aggressive compression. These results suggest that the learned spectral basis better prioritizes informative directions, enabling more effective representation compression. Notably, SpecMRL extends the performance frontier of MRL, surpassing full-dimensional MRL with only half the embedding dimensions.

Такой подход делает первые компоненты вектора более информативными для сжатого представления. Abstract MemPilot uses reinforcement learning to dynamically curate multimodal agent memory, balancing performance, cost, and latency through adaptive retrieval and model selection.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Memory has become integral to the LLM agent ecosystem, supporting information retention and reuse across interactions. However, most existing agent memory systems construct memory in a query-agnostic manner, which can incur unnecessary preprocessing cost and discard details that later prove essential. Recent studies have begun shifting memory processing toward runtime adaptation, but typically specialize in particular operations or fixed processing schemes, leaving flexible control over performance, cost, and latency largely underexplored. To address this challenge, we present MemPilot, a flexible framework that orchestrates on-demand memory curation under different performance--cost--latency preferences. Specifically, we optimize a multi-step LLM policy via reinforcement learning to iteratively choose between retrieving from query-agnostic memory and delegating query-specific curation of raw multimodal history to heterogeneous LLMs and VLMs. The policy jointly controls evidence amount, curation instructions, model selection, and visual access, enabling fine-grained allocation of runtime computation. To optimize this policy under competing objectives, we adapt objective-wise advantage decoupling by separately estimating each objective's advantage before aggregation. Moreover, we introduce prefix-based marginal utility estimation for fine-grained credit assignment across multi-step rollouts. Experiments on five multimodal agent-memory benchmarks demonstrate favorable performance--cost--latency trade-offs across optimization preferences, with preference sweeps yielding broader frontiers than existing trade-off-aware baselines.

Вместо построения памяти без учёта запроса, политика агента на каждом шаге решает, извлекать ли данные из общей памяти или делегировать курирование сырой истории LLM и VLM, при этом выбирая объём доказательств, инструкции, модель и доступ к изображениям. Abstract RoboValue introduces a benchmark to evaluate robotic value models across task-state understanding, progress tracking, failure recovery, and consistency in both simulated and real-world settings.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Robotic value models assess task execution from visual observations and instructions, providing feedback for policy learning and execution monitoring. However, existing evaluations cover only selected aspects of execution understanding, leaving the reliability of value feedback insufficiently tested. We introduce RoboValue, a fine-grained sim-and-real benchmark for unified evaluation of robotic value models. Its framework evaluates four complementary capability dimensions through shared interfaces: task-state understanding, temporal progress monitoring, failure and recovery reasoning, and value consistency. Diagnostic trajectories and instruction variations test whether value judgments accurately reflect task requirements and changes throughout execution. RoboValue-Dataset covers 15 simulation and 20 real-world manipulation tasks, providing expert demonstrations for training and annotated trajectories for fine-grained evaluation. We evaluate representative robotic value models in zero-shot and one-shot settings under standard conditions and separate shifts in robot embodiment and environment. Our results reveal errors in fine-grained instruction grounding, history-dependent progress tracking, and recovery assessment, even in models with strong outcome and forward-progress scores. The same semantic subtask can also receive substantially different value gains across valid execution orders. These findings identify concrete targets for value supervision, while the dataset and public leaderboard support continued assessment of feedback reliability. The website is available at https://robovalue-benchmark.github.io/.

Abstract KronLSTM introduces a Kronecker Delta Rule for stable associative memory updates in linear attention, improving long-context modeling without extra normalization.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small We propose KronLSTM, a linear attention architecture based on the Kronecker Delta Rule, a novel update rule for associative matrix memories. KronLSTM extends the xLSTM family with a memory-revision mechanism that corrects targeted associations while modifying the memory only along a single direction in matrix space. This minimally invasive memory revision reduces interference with previously stored information compared with delta-rule updates, making it particularly well suited for long-context language modeling. The resulting memory dynamics can be mathematically described as a Markov chain that converges to a steady-state distribution, while the expected norm of the memory matrix remains uniformly bounded throughout the entire input sequence. Hence, KronLSTM ensures stable memory dynamics without requiring additional normalization and stabilization mechanisms for the memory state. Experiments show that KronLSTM is competitive with, and often outperforms, existing linear attention architectures, including models of the (Gated) DeltaNet family, with particularly strong performance on long context benchmarks. These results establish the Kronecker Delta Rule as an effective and analytically tractable foundation for stable associative memory in recurrent linear attention architectures. Our code (https://github.com/NX-AI/KronLSTM) and models (https://huggingface.co/collections/NX-AI/kronlstm) are open-source.
[20.10.2026 10:47] Response: {"desc": "В статье представлен KronLSTM — архитектура линейного внимания (linear attention), основанная на новом правиле обновления ассоциативной матричной памяти, Kronecker Delta Rule. Правило корректирует целевые ассоциации, изменяя память только вдоль одного направления в пространстве матриц, что снижает интерференцию с ранее сохранённой информацией по сравнению с delta-rule. Динамика памяти описывается как марковская цепь, сходящаяся к стационарному распределению, а ожидаемая норма матрицы памяти равномерно ограничена, поэтому дополнительная нормализация не нужна. Эксперименты показывают, что KronLSTM конкурентоспособна с моделями семейств (Gated) DeltaNet и часто их превосходит, особенно на бенчмарках с длинным контекстом.", "emoji": "🧠", "title": "Стабильная память без лишней нормализации для длинных контекстов"}

Abstract Physical recursive self-improvement in vision-language agents is evaluated via multi-axis metrics, scaling analysis, and workspace interventions to assess improvement speed, limits, and reusable experience.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small In this paper, we systematically investigate the mechanisms, scaling behavior, and generalization of physical Recursive self-improvement. We introduce PhysRSI-eval to quantify physical RSI through a five-axis metric: First-Attempt Performance, Efficiency, Success, Budget and Improvement from failure. Different VLMs are evaluated on 18 manipulation tasks under a fixed interaction budget. The analysis of experimental results motivates the Physical Barrel Hypothesis: different capabilities may constrain different parts of improvement. The five-axis metrics also distinguish systems with close public benchmark averages and show that high scores need not come with early or efficient gains. We study observational scaling from two perspectives: predicting the relative performance of VLM agents from their public capabilities, and forecasting later progress from early physical feedback. Together, these analyses ask how quickly and how far an agent can improve. Moreover, we conduct workspace interventions and then analyze what improvement leaves behind. Removing history reduces late-round success, frozen workspaces remain useful on unseen layouts, and three cross-task cases achieve success earlier. The results suggest that scene-specific adaptation and reusable experience can coexist.
[20.10.2026 10:48] Response: {"desc": "Статья исследует физическое рекурсивное самоулучшение (physical RSI) у vision-language агентов, которые управляют роботом в задачах манипуляции. Авторы вводят метрику PhysRSI-eval с пятью осями: качество первой попытки, эффективность, успешность, бюджет взаимодействия и улучшение после неудач, и оценивают VLM на 18 задачах при фиксированном бюджете. Гипотеза Physical Barrel Hypothesis утверждает, что разные способности ограничивают разные части процесса улучшения, а наблюдательный scaling-анализ позволяет прогнозировать прогресс агента по раннему физическому фидбэку. Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small In this paper, we systematically investigate the mechanisms, scaling behavior, and generalization of physical Recursive self-improvement. We introduce PhysRSI-eval to quantify physical RSI through a five-axis metric: First-Attempt Performance, Efficiency, Success, Budget and Improvement from failure. Different VLMs are evaluated on 18 manipulation tasks under a fixed interaction budget. The analysis of experimental results motivates the Physical Barrel Hypothesis: different capabilities may constrain different parts of improvement. The five-axis metrics also distinguish systems with close public benchmark averages and show that high scores need not come with early or efficient gains. We study observational scaling from two perspectives: predicting the relative performance of VLM agents from their public capabilities, and forecasting later progress from early physical feedback. Together, these analyses ask how quickly and how far an agent can improve. Moreover, we conduct workspace interventions and then analyze what improvement leaves behind. Removing history reduces late-round success, frozen workspaces remain useful on unseen layouts, and three cross-task cases achieve success earlier. The results suggest that scene-specific adaptation and reusable experience can coexist."

Abstract Sauron is an evidence-first agentic verifier that independently validates claims against environment state and execution traces to improve rubric-based evaluation accuracy and reduce reinforcement-learning reward errors.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Long-horizon, semi-verifiable agentic tasks often rely on rubric-based evaluation, but the evidence needed to assess these tasks can be spread across the environment state and the execution trace. A judge may overlook this evidence or accept unsupported claims, including an agent's own reports of success. When rubric verdicts serve as reinforcement-learning rewards, such errors can reinforce undesirable behavior. We introduce Sauron, an evidence-first agentic verifier that investigates both state and trace and relies on a claim only after independently validating it against its source. Bounded queries and joint investigation of related criteria reduce context use and redundant work, and each verdict carries an auditable receipt of its supporting evidence. Across 2,586 human-labeled criteria from 100 personal-assistant episodes, Sauron achieves the highest F1 of five verifiers on all eight backbones tested while costing less than Gandalf, the strongest baseline. With GPT-5.6 Sol, it reaches 0.96 F1 versus Gandalf's 0.92 at 23% lower cost, with a wider margin on process criteria. On a paired fault-injection suite covering 13 families, several of which simulate reward hacking, Sauron detects 97% of injected failures while flagging only 2% of clean controls, outperforming baselines even when they are given the full trace. On TRAIL, a benchmark for detecting and localizing real agent errors, Sauron leads both Gandalf and TRAIL's own judge on all three quality metrics.
[20.10.2026 10:48] Response: {"desc": "Статья представляет Sauron — агентного верификатора, который проверяет утверждения агента, опираясь на состояние окружения и трассу выполнения, а не принимая отчёты агента на веру. Это важно для rubric-based оценки и reinforcement learning, где ошибочные награды могут закреплять нежелательное поведение, например reward hacking. Модель использует ограниченные запросы, совместную проверку связанных критериев и аудируемые receipts с доказательствами для каждого вердикта. На 2 586 размеченных людьми критериях Sauron показывает лучший F1 среди пяти верификаторов и обнаруживает 97% инжектированных ошибок при 2% ложных срабатываний.", "emoji": "🔍", "title": "Не верь на слово: проверяй доказательства, а не отчёты агента"}

Abstract BlenderWorld is a benchmark for evaluating multimodal language models on realistic 3D creation tasks using rendered outputs and binary rubrics, revealing significant gaps in spatial coherence.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Recent advances in multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have enabled them to act as autonomous agents, creating and editing 3D models via code or direct software manipulation. In practical workflows, these models must infer object shapes, materials, spatial layouts, and lighting from diverse references while strictly following instructions to reconstruct, modify, or design 3D content. However, existing benchmarks provide limited coverage of such open-ended requests across real-world scenarios. To bridge this gap, we introduce BlenderWorld, the first comprehensive benchmark dedicated to evaluating frontier models on realistic 3D creation tasks. BlenderWorld comprises 223 tasks spanning architecture, products, natural environments, and scientific visualization, driven by text, image, video, and structured specification inputs. To rigorously assess these complex deliverables, we establish a unified evaluation protocol: we render the submitted Blender projects from multiple viewpoints and evaluate them against 2,651 task-specific binary rubrics alongside holistic visual quality scores. Benchmarking thirteen frontier models reveals that GPT-6-Astra achieves the highest overall fused score of 79.9, followed by Opus-5 (73.0) and GPT-5.6-Sol (67.7). Ultimately, our findings reveal a critical bottleneck: while current models can parse broad instructions, they still profoundly struggle with fine-grained 3D spatial coherence and accurate structural reproduction in Blender, leaving substantial headroom for future 3D-native agentics.

To rigorously assess these complex deliverables, we establish a unified evaluation protocol: we render the submitted Blender projects from multiple viewpoints and evaluate them against 2,651 task-specific binary rubrics alongside holistic visual quality scores. Benchmarking thirteen frontier models reveals that GPT-6-Astra achieves the highest overall fused score of 79.9, followed by Opus-5 (73.0) and GPT-5.6-Sol (67.7). Ultimately, our findings reveal a critical bottleneck: while current models can parse broad instructions, they still profoundly struggle with fine-grained 3D spatial coherence and accurate structural reproduction in Blender, leaving substantial headroom for future 3D-native agentics."

Abstract MARS enables multimodal safety steering using only text-derived refusal directions, avoiding the need for multimodal safety data.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Safety in Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) depends on unsafe multimodal data for post-training alignment or activation steering. However, such data is hard to collect, must be gathered anew for each modality, and its collection and distribution raise ethical and legal concerns. In this work, we relax this constraint and investigate whether textual refusal directions, extracted directly from the LLM backbone, generalize across modalities (i.e., image, video). Preliminary findings confirm this ability, though effectiveness is conditioned by layer selection, steering strength, and cross-modal alignment, with the latter causing safe multimodal inputs to be spuriously steered toward refusal. Building on this, we introduce Modality-Agnostic Refusal Steering (MARS), a lightweight training-free approach that injects multimodal safety without the need for multimodal safety data. MARS corrects modality misalignment via activation centering, adaptively scales steering strength within a geometrically defined trust region, and selects the optimal intervention layer, operating at the first generated token. Evaluated on five SOTA MLLMs across safety, utility, and video jailbreak, MARS achieves consistent safety gains while preserving utility up to over-refusal. MARS reveals that safety-relevant structure is shared across modalities and that textual refusal directions are a powerful and underexplored foundation for multimodal alignment.

Авторы предлагают MARS — метод без обучения, который корректирует сдвиг модальностей через activation centering, адаптивно масштабирует силу воздействия в геометрически заданной trust region и выбирает оптимальный слой для вмешательства. MARS применяется на первом генерируемом токене и не требует мультимодальных данных с небезопасным контентом. Abstract LIBERO-Industry introduces a benchmark translating industrial manufacturing constraints into controllable robot learning tasks to evaluate generalist policies under realistic production conditions.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Industrial manipulation in flexible manufacturing, e.g., high-mix, low-volume (HMLV) electronics assembly, requires robot policies to adapt to changing products and production conditions. Such settings impose industrial process constraints, including product variants, moving material flows, finite execution windows, and tight geometric tolerances. However, existing evaluation frameworks do not systematically capture these constraints when assessing generalist robot policies. To address this gap, we introduce LIBERO-Industry, a novel benchmark built on the widely used robot learning benchmark LIBERO, that translates the aforementioned industrial constraints into controllable task conditions and measurable success criteria. Specifically, LIBERO-Industry comprises three classic task scenarios: Static-to-Dynamic (S2D) loading, Dynamic-to-Static (D2S) unloading, and Dynamic-to-Dynamic (D2D) transfer. The suites contain 216 tasks in total that systematically vary object geometry, conveyor speed, interaction-window duration, and geometric acceptance region size. Extensive experiments across five generalist robot policies (including vision-language-action models and world-action models) show that performance progressively degrades as industrial constraints are introduced and combined. Under a shared fine-tuning and evaluation protocol, the LIBERO-Industry co-training condition achieved higher observed target-transfer completion than the original-LIBERO condition.
[20.10.2026 10:48] Response: {"desc": "В статье представлен LIBERO-Industry — новый бенчмарк для оценки универсальных роботизированных политик (generalist policies) в условиях реального промышленного производства. Он переводит ограничения гибкого производства, например сборки электроники малыми сериями, в управляемые задачи robot learning с измеримыми критериями успеха. Бенчмарк включает 216 задач трёх типов: загрузка со статичного на динамический конвейер, выгрузка с динамического на статичный и передача между динамическими конвейерами, варьируя геометрию объектов, скорость конвейера, длительность окна взаимодействия и размер допустимой области. Эксперименты с пятью моделями, включая VLA и world-action модели, показали, что качество падает по мере добавления и комбинирования ограничений, а co-training на LIBERO-Industry даёт лучший результат переноса на целевые задачи.", "emoji": "🏭", "title": "Проверяем роботов-манипуляторов на реальных условиях фабрики, а не только в лаборатории"}

"Abstract LIBERO-Industry introduces a benchmark translating industrial manufacturing constraints into controllable robot learning tasks to evaluate generalist policies under realistic production conditions.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Industrial manipulation in flexible manufacturing, e.g., high-mix, low-volume (HMLV) electronics assembly, requires robot policies to adapt to changing products and production conditions. Such settings impose industrial process constraints, including product variants, moving material flows, finite execution windows, and tight geometric tolerances. However, existing evaluation frameworks do not systematically capture these constraints when assessing generalist robot policies. To address this gap, we introduce LIBERO-Industry, a novel benchmark built on the widely used robot learning benchmark LIBERO, that translates the aforementioned industrial constraints into controllable task conditions and measurable success criteria. Specifically, LIBERO-Industry comprises three classic task scenarios: Static-to-Dynamic (S2D) loading, Dynamic-to-Static (D2S) unloading, and Dynamic-to-Dynamic (D2D) transfer. The suites contain 216 tasks in total that systematically vary object geometry, conveyor speed, interaction-window duration, and geometric acceptance region size. Extensive experiments across five generalist robot policies (including vision-language-action models and world-action models) show that performance progressively degrades as industrial constraints are introduced and combined. Under a shared fine-tuning and evaluation protocol, the LIBERO-Industry co-training condition achieved higher observed target-transfer completion than the original-LIBERO condition."

Abstract ActionRoPE encodes action-driven camera motion into world-coordinate rotary positions for consistent spatial control, paired with coordinate-conditioned noise scheduling to stabilize observed regions before generating new content.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Game world models have made rapid progress toward real-time interactive generation. Yet action control is still predominantly implemented as feature conditioning, leaving the model to learn from training data how each action should move the visual world. This formulation is poorly matched to a broad class of planar-camera worlds, such as top-down and isometric games. In these worlds, player motion induces a geometrically well-defined planar camera displacement. We observe that the key to spatially consistent control in such worlds is to represent the effect of each action directly in world coordinates. To this end, we introduce ActionRoPE, a parameter-free action interface that converts action-driven camera displacement into world-coordinate rotary positions. As a result, tokens corresponding to the same world location retain the same positional address across time. These world coordinates further expose a spatial asymmetry in generation: regions covered by the initial observation are geometrically known, while newly revealed regions remain to be synthesized. We therefore propose Coordinate-Conditioned Noise Scheduling (CNS), which assigns token-wise denoising schedules according to this known-new structure. It allows observed regions to stabilize earlier, while new content is generated with reference to them. Compared with four representative action-conditioning mechanisms, our approach follows the commanded motion more faithfully and reduces the revisit drift by 66% over the strongest baseline. The same interface transfers across video backbones from 1.3B to 33B parameters and to out-of-distribution scenes. Project Page: https://teawhite.cn/ActionRoPE/
[20.10.2026 10:48] Response: {"desc": "Авторы предлагают ActionRoPE — параметрически бесплатный интерфейс, который переводит смещение камеры, вызванное действиями игрока, в мировые координаты для rotary positional embeddings. Это позволяет токенам одной и той же точки мира сохранять одинаковый позиционный адрес во времени, что особенно важно для планарных игр вроде top-down и изометрических. Дополнительно метод Coordinate-Conditioned Noise Scheduling (CNS) назначает токенам разные расписания денойзинга: уже наблюдаемые области стабилизируются раньше, а новые генерируются с опорой на них. По сравнению с четырьмя базовыми механизмами action-conditioning подход точнее следует командам движения и снижает дрейф при повторных посещениях на 66%, при этом хорошо переносится на видео-бэкбоны от 1.3B до 33B параметров.", "emoji": "🎮", "title": "Действие задаёт координаты: игровой мир, который не плывёт"}

Abstract UniAVC trains separate audio and video branches on abundant unimodal data before aligning them with scarce paired data, using independent noise schedules and asymmetric supervision to enable joint synthesis, cross-modal generation, and instruction-guided editing.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Unified audio-video creation requires a single model capable of text-conditioned joint synthesis, bidirectional cross-modal generation, and instruction-guided editing across both modalities. Existing methods rely heavily on paired corpora, which are scarce for joint generation and even rarer for editing. Consequently, they are spent learning single-branch generative and editing capabilities that abundant unimodal data could readily teach, rather than on the cross-modal coupling. Furthermore, prevailing systems apply a single shared noise schedule to both modalities, neglecting two critical limitations. First, due to the significant discrepancy in information density, video requires prolonged high noise to organize spatial and motion structure, whereas the sparser audio signal can settle earlier during the synthesis process, the dynamics a uniform schedule might compromise. Second, lockstep denoising leaves asymmetric workflows, such as conditioning on one modality to synthesize or edit another, untrained. We present UniAVC, which addresses both obstacles. It first trains each branch on unimodal dataset to full generative and editing competence, and subsequently dedicates paired data to exploit cross-modal correspondence. Modality-decoupled schedulers meanwhile warp the noise schedule independently from a shared timestep, while subtasks with one clean modality directly supervise asymmetric configurations. These settings respectively enable intent-aware editing, which preserves one modality intact while enlisting it to constrain the other, and post-inference self-refinement, which regenerates only an unsatisfactory branch against an accepted one. UniAVC thus spans multiple creation tasks from a single set of weights without task-specific adapters.
[20.10.2026 10:48] Response: {"desc": "UniAVC — это унифицированная модель для создания аудио и видео, которая обучает отдельные ветви для аудио и видео на большом объёме однородных (unimodal) данных, а затем выравнивает их с помощью ограниченного набора парных данных. Модель использует независимые расписания шума (noise schedules) для каждой модальности, что учитывает разную информационную плотность видео и аудио. Асимметричное обучение с одной чистой модальностью позволяет решать задачи редактирования по инструкции и самоуточнения после инференса. Всё это реализуется одним набором весов без task-specific адаптеров, охватывая синтез, кросс-модальную генерацию и редактирование.", "emoji": "🎬", "title": "Одна модель для аудио и видео: учим по отдельности, связываем вместе"}

Abstract FineSplat improves feed-forward 3D Gaussian Splatting by using fine-grained patches, dual grouping for efficient multi-view interaction, rendering-based feedback, and compact Gaussian generation to recover richer geometry and appearance efficiently.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Feed-forward 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) promises instant scene reconstruction with high-quality novel-view synthesis. Yet, fine-grained geometry and appearance remain difficult to recover, motivating us to revisit how feed-forward models represent, aggregate, and refine detailed visual information. We introduce FineSplat, a feed-forward 3DGS framework built around fine-grained image patches. Since global multi-view attention becomes prohibitively expensive at such high token resolutions, we develop a dual-grouping strategy that combines two complementary forms of locality: view-wise grouping enables efficient interaction among neighboring views, while 3D spatial grouping reconnects geometrically related tokens that may never have interacted under view-wise grouping by lifting them into the 3D scene space. We further leverage reconstruction error as direct feedback, providing an explicit rendering-based signal to recover fine-grained structures and appearance, thereby overcoming the limitations of one-pass feed-forward prediction. Finally, a compact Gaussian generation scheme preserves the benefits of fine-grained features without unnecessarily increasing the number of primitives. Together, these designs enable FineSplat to recover richer geometry and appearance while remaining efficient and compact, achieving state-of-the-art results by a substantial margin, under the same or smaller Gaussian budgets than existing methods.
[20.10.2026 10:48] Response: {"desc": "Статья представляет FineSplat — feed-forward фреймворк для 3D Gaussian Splatting, который работает с мелкозернистыми патчами изображений, чтобы восстанавливать более детальную геометрию и внешний вид сцены. Чтобы избежать непомерных затрат глобального multi-view attention на высоком разрешении токенов, авторы используют dual-grouping: view-wise группировку для соседних видов и 3D spatial группировку, которая связывает геометрически близкие токены в пространстве сцены. Ошибка реконструкции используется как явный rendering-based сигнал обратной связи, что позволяет уточнять мелкие структуры и преодолевать ограничения одношагового предсказания. Abstract ECHO integrates multi-horizon prediction into the backbone via self-recursion and dynamic depth sampling, improving pretraining efficiency and inference drafting without auxiliary modules.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Existing Multi-token prediction (MTP) methods are auxiliary by design: the multi-horizon loss mostly trains modules attached to the backbone, and each added horizon costs another module and another pass. We propose Efficient Causal Self-Recursion (ECHO), which removes both costs. The backbone's own final layer predicts every horizon, so the supervision lands on the parameters that generate the next token, and Dynamic Depth Sampling (DDS) keeps deeper horizons affordable by drawing the training depth with probabilities that decay along depth. Future prediction thus becomes part of the autoregressive objective rather than a task attached to it, and the gains follow on both sides of it. In pretraining, added depth converts into downstream accuracy where auxiliary modules saturate, concentrating on reasoning and code at half the MTP training overhead of training every depth. At inference, the pretrained model is its own drafter: it drafts beyond the depth it was trained for, and keeps its draft-target alignment on the open-ended, high-entropy generation where post-hoc drafters lose theirs. The advantage survives a doubled token budget, a repeated pretraining run, and supervised finetuning.
[20.10.2026 10:49] Response: {"desc": "В статье представлен метод ECHO, который встраивает предсказание нескольких будущих токенов прямо в основную модель, используя её последний слой вместо отдельных вспомогательных модулей. Для экономии вычислений применяется Dynamic Depth Sampling (DDS), который выбирает глубину обучения с убывающими вероятностями. Такой подход улучшает качество предобучения, особенно в задачах рассуждения и написания кода, при вдвое меньших затратах по сравнению с полным multi-token prediction (MTP). На этапе инференса модель сама выступает в роли drafter для speculative decoding, сохраняя согласованность предсказаний даже в открытой генерации.", "emoji": "🔮", "title": "Модель учится предсказывать будущее сама, без лишних модулей"}

Abstract Researchers benchmark vision-language models on simulated physical work tasks, finding strengths in text-based documentation but weaknesses in measurement reading, spatial reasoning, and acceptability judgment.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Augmenting worker capabilities in the physical world through guidance has been a decades-long goal for AI agents. One key supporting capability is to understand what the worker is doing, seeing through the human's camera or smart glasses, and assist them in documentation. Despite advances in vision language models, their capabilities in these settings remain uncertain and are challenging to benchmark because most physical work data is proprietary, and involves too much variability to study in a principled manner. Unlike structured settings such as kitchens and warehouses that have predictable lighting, geometry, and objects, field-facing tasks such as rooftop solar installation and vehicle repair are unconstrained and high variance. Here, we model real-world physical work in Blender across eleven environments, incorporating variants in both image acceptability and documentation. The acceptability task asks whether a particular view would be suitable proof-of-work and documentation asks whether an agent can understand the task-relevant state of the environment from that view. Across 26 vision-language models, we find that top models largely solve documentation when the answer is text in frame, but are weak at reading measurements and at judging small parts and spatial relations, such as whether a tape measure leans. Acceptability is harder to pin down because human raters disagree with each other. Measured against a scorer that agrees with the raters' majority about as often as a single rater does, the best models cannot be distinguished from a single non-author rater, at a cost within the range of a human pass. In the three environments where we can trace acceptability errors, most wrong rejections come from misreading the scene rather than the requirement. On the three documentation questions for which we have real images, model rankings on rendered frames transfer to real images.

Модели проверяются на two задачи: оценка приемлемости кадра как proof-of-work и понимание состояния среды для документирования. Лучшие модели хорошо справляются с чтением текста в кадре, но плохо считывают показания измерительных приборов и распознают мелкие детали и пространственные отношения. Оценка acceptability осложняется расхождением человеческих разметчиков, а результаты на рендерах в основном переносятся на реальные изображения.", "emoji": "🛠️", "title": "Видят ли VLM-модели то, что делает рабочий?"}

Abstract Autoregressive transformers retain prompt influence via a power-law decay driven by attention dilution and relay mechanisms, with trained models forgetting faster than untrained ones.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small We ask how much an autoregressive transformer retains of its prompt after generating t tokens. Consider two different prompts followed by the same continuation text, so that any difference in their hidden states comes from the prompt alone, and we find that the distance between their hidden states decays approximately as a power law t^{-α}, where α=1 is the rate at which a fixed prompt's share of uniform attention shrinks and smaller values mean longer memory. Trained models forget fast (αapprox0.55--0.7), untrained ones slowly (αapprox0.2--0.5). We prove that this offset is Obig(P(log t)^{L-1}/tbig) for a prompt of length P and depth L. The bound holds for finite-depth pre-LN softmax transformers with bounded attention logits, under one additional condition: the pre-norm activations stay away from zero. The mechanism is geometric: the prompt's direct influence is diluted as the context grows, and relay --- continuation tokens passing on what earlier tokens read from the prompt --- can only add logarithmic factors. This is what we call the conditional no-hair theorem: given the text generated so far, the prompt's influence on the next token vanishes as t grows, so any property of the prompt that survives, such as its language, must be carried by the text itself. An exactly solvable linear relay model shows that the bound cannot be improved and reproduces the rise of the local exponent towards 1 seen in untrained networks. Cancelling the dilution with a logit bias stops the decay early in training and reverses it after, so dilution is necessary for the forgetting we measure; untrained networks and the solvable model show it is also sufficient.
[20.10.2026 10:49] Response: {"desc": "Статья исследует, сколько информации о промпте сохраняет авторегрессивный трансформер после генерации t токенов. Авторы показывают, что различие скрытых состояний для двух промптов с одинаковым продолжением убывает по степенному закону t^{-α}, причём обученные модели забывают промпт быстрее необученных. Доказана верхняя оценка влияния промпта, основанная на размывании внимания (attention dilution) и эффекте релейной передачи (relay) между токенами продолжения. Эксперименты и точно решаемая линейная модель подтверждают, что размывание внимания является и необходимым, и достаточным условием такого забывания.", "emoji": "🧠", "title": "Промпт постепенно растворяется в тексте: чем дальше генерация, тем слабее его влияние"}
